#2907. XPeng: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Licensing its Turing AI chips and software to outside automakers could add a second revenue stream beyond selling cars, a point management tied directly to margins.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 18, 2026 at 6:50PM EST

On its third quarter of 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 16, 2026, XPeng (XPEV -2.50%) announced that vehicle deliveries reached 118,390 units in the third quarter. Accelerating overseas sales and commercial technology licensing are creating high-margin growth channels beyond domestic vehicle manufacturing. Three things management said stand out.

Overseas deliveries generate higher profit margins per vehicle than domestic sales in China. International sales hit a record in October as overseas deliveries of MONA L03 began.

XPeng is preparing to mass-produce IRON, its general-purpose humanoid robot, for commercial deployment in its own stores and with outside customers in 2027.

Through its XPeng AI Engineering unit, the company aims to offer its custom Turing artificial intelligence semiconductor chips, foundation models, and autonomous driving technology to other companies.

Monthly vehicle deliveries hit a record in October, with overseas sales also setting a record. Expanding local manufacturing overseas mitigates trade tariffs while preserving pricing discipline and tech-driven brand positioning.

Disciplined research and development spending supports core EV operations while funding expansion into physical artificial intelligence.

#2903. Yes, You Can Invest in Grand Theft Auto VI (GTA 6). Here's How.

Investors have several options for buying GTA 6-related investments.

By Will Healy – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Take-Two Interactive Software ›

The release of the Grand Theft Auto VI (GTA 6) video game is finally upon us. No new version of this extremely popular interactive digital entertainment has come out since the release of GTA 5 in 2013, and since the release of the first iteration of the game in 1997, it has ranked as one of the best-selling gaming franchises of all time.

Not surprisingly, the anticipation has drawn the interest of gamers and investors alike. Fortunately, the GTA 6 release presents an opportunity for shareholders; here's how investors can capitalize.

The most straightforward way to invest in this game is to buy Take-Two Interactive (TTWO +6.22%) stock. Take-Two is the parent company of Rockstar Games, the studio that developed the game. The game's launch brings to mind multiple takeaways for long-term investors, raised just after the company released its latest earnings report. Take-Two stock rose by 6.6% in the trading session that closed hours before the game's release. However, the stock has dropped over the last year largely due to the company's past delays in releasing the game.

In the first half of fiscal 2027 (ended Sept. 30), revenue of $3 billion fell by 8% from year-ago levels. Analysts blamed weaker in-game spending on NBA 2K and the anticipation of GTA 6 as reasons for the decline.

Also, the company reported an $88 million loss during the same period, though analysts forecast a return to profitability in upcoming quarters. For now, Take-Two's price-to-sales (P/S) ratio is 6.3. That is slightly above the five-year average of 5.3 and suggests investors will pay a modest premium by buying the stock now.

Amid that high valuation, some investors might not be comfortable owning Take-Two stock. Others might see the GTA 6 release as an opportunity for gaming in general. Such investors may choose to invest through an exchange-traded fund (ETF), with multiple opportunities available.

The ETF with the highest exposure to Take-Two is the VanEck Digital Native Economy ETF (GENZ +0.77%). Approximately 8.25% of the fund's holdings are in Take-Two. Unfortunately, this comes with a 0.51% expense ratio, just above the industry average of 0.48%. Moreover, it lost 26% of its value over the last five years, further dampening its appeal.

The one with the best five-year performance is the VanEck Video Gaming and eSports ETF (ESPO +1.55%), which returned about 35% over that time. Its exposure to Take-Two is slightly less, just 6.05%. Still, the higher return probably makes the 0.55% expense ratio more palatable.

Another fund to consider is the State Street Communication Services Select SPDR ETF (XLC +0.30%). It returned 31% over the last five years, and its 0.08% expense ratio makes it cheap to own. Still, Take-Two makes up just 4.75% of the fund's holdings, and most of the stocks are not in gaming.

As one of the best-selling gaming franchises of all time, the GTA 6 release looks to be huge, and investors have many options to capitalize on the opportunity.

Investing in the launch of GTA 6 involves holding a position in Rockstar Games' parent company, Take-Two, either directly or through an ETF. Whatever direction investors take, the revival of one of gaming's most popular franchises is an opportunity that does not come along often.

Knowing that, investors probably have good reason to at least consider investments tied to the communication stock and, possibly, the gaming industry in general.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 794%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 203% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

#2899. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2890. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2883. Federal Reserve issues FOMC statement

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2877. How three emergency centers helped Lakers survive the Hornets without Walker Kessler

One had fallen out of the rotation. Another was playing out of position. The last one hasn'tlearned the entire playbook yet.

The Lakers' trio of emergency centers helped the team eke out a 118-114 overtime win against the Charlotte Hornets on Tuesday as starter Walker Kessler sat out because of leg soreness. The Lakers (13-2) confronted their biggest roster flaw with Kevon Looney, Jarred Vanderbilt and two-way center Carson Cooper to battle one of the best rebounding teams in the league, claw back from an 18-point third-quarter deficit and extend their winning streak to nine games.

"Tonight showed how important those guys are," guard Collin Sexton said, "but also how important [it is] to stay ready. And one thing about Vando and Kevon, they're always going to stay ready, even if they may not see the floor the night before, they're always gonna stay ready."

The Lakers' lack of depth at center behind Kessler was such a glaring weakness that the front office spent all of training camp trying to execute a trade to acquire a reliable backup center. With Kessler averaging 14.5 points and 10.9 rebounds on 68% shooting, it seemed easy to overlook the dearth of options behind him as the Lakers rocketed to the top of the standings.

Read more: Quentin Grimes scores 47 points, propels Lakers to 'statement' win over Hornets

That wasn't possible Tuesday when the center was ruled out because of lower right leg soreness. His absence was primarily out of precaution, Lakers coach JJ Redick said, ahead of a second game in as many nights.

Looney, who didn't play in eight of the last 10 games and hadn't played more than five minutes in a game since the season opener, started in Kessler's place. He had a season-high eight rebounds with four points. Looney set the tone with three rebounds in the first 70 seconds.

Vanderbilt was among the first group of substitutions and delivered seven rebounds in 13 minutes and 41 seconds. His team-high six offensive rebounds paced the Lakers to a season-high-tying 19 offensive rebounds that led to 19 second-chance points.

"In a game like this, where you don't have your best stuff, and they're junking the game up, the scoring opportunity stuff matters," Redick said. "And I thought between the offensive rebounding and limiting our turnovers, us turning them over, we won that battle."

The Lakers had 14 turnovers, their fourth-lowest of the season, and forced 20. The Hornets, the only team in the NBA with a fourth-quarter net rating worse than the Lakers, committed 11 turnovers in the fourth quarter and overtime.

Read more: JJ Redick not pleased after Lakers nearly blow 31-point lead to Suns

The Hornets (4-10) entered Tuesday as the top offensive-rebounding team in the league with 15.2 per game, and center Moussa Diabate led the league with 5.5 offensive rebounds per game. But the Lakers, who ranked 27th in defensive rebounds, limited Charlotte to 12 offensive rebounds.

Cooper had four rebounds and a career-high three points in 18 minutes and three seconds, the most playing time of the three Lakers centers. He had a critical offensive rebound with 4:26 remaining in the fourth when he tipped the ball back to guard Quentin Grimes, who hit a three-pointer to pull the Lakers to within one point. Grimes scored a career-high 47 points, including 10 made threes, and nailed a three-pointer that forced overtime.

Cooper signed a two-way deal a week ago then found himself in the vital fourth-quarter group that whittled the lead down to four before Luka Doncic subbed back in. Doncic figured the 22-year-old rookie was a little bit nervous.

Cooper hadn't practiced with the Lakers since signing on Nov. 10. He played in one NBA game while on a two-way contract with the Memphis Grizzlies this season. He was still watching film with assistant coach and head of player development Zach Guthrie in the locker room before the game to get caught up on the Lakers' playbook.

Redick said coaches would try to simplify the play calls for the second unit, asking Cooper to know only three specific plays if he was on the court with Doncic.

Read more: While tied for NBA's best record, Lakers believe they can be better

"He understands the assignment," Redick said. "Not to get too, like, big picture with what the G League is and what the two-ways are, but I think philosophically we've gone in a bunch of different directions with our two-ways. And I think with this group, like we really wanted to get guys that could play a role for this team if needed. … He's here to play a role, and it's not, 'Oh, this guy can go score 30 points in a G League game.' That doesn't necessarily equate to what we need right now."

Cooper was a four-year player at Michigan State but didn't crack the starting lineup until his senior season. He helped the Spartans reach the NCAA regional semifinal only eight months ago.

On Tuesday, he was suddenly starting in overtime for the Lakers. The tallest available player on the Lakers roster promptly won the tip, he proudly reminded reporters in the locker room.

Sign up for our weekly newsletter on all things Lakers.

This story originally appeared in Los Angeles Times.

#2870. Raptors seek end to skid against faultering Wizards

The Toronto Raptors have struggled through their last stretch of games. Dropping games to Memphis, Boston, Philly, and New York twice has resulted in a five game skid that contributes to a forming pattern of the Raptors' inability to beat good teams this season.

Luckily for Toronto, tonight could be a good respite from this as they face the 4-11 Washington Wizards who have just dropped eight straight games.

In their first matchup of the season, Toronto handled Washington easily, coming away with a 112-102 victory, led by Scottie Barnes who had a 28-point, 14-rebound double-double. Washington's best performer was Trae Young who had a 22-point, 12 assist double-double of his own, but didn't have enough help around him to give the Raptors any trouble.

It wasn't a pretty victory either. The Raptors shot a mere 21% from the field, with RJ, Scottie, and IQ combining for an abysmal 2-17 from long range. They also had 16 turnovers, with Scottie personally committing 8 of those. In most situations, those kind of numbers would likely mean a loss, but Toronto managed to beat the Wizards in other ways, which they will have to do again to get out of this slump.

Toronto dominated the boards in their first contest, outrebounding Washington 53-40. Removing second chance opportunities goes a long way to building some separation. Even more helpful though were the 19 of those that were offensive rebounds, creating their own second chance opportunities to prevent empty possessions.

When you're making mistakes and turning the ball over, you need to make sure you are putting in that much more effort on the defensive end to try and win the possession back. Collin Murray-Boyles, who has been having a phenomenal season so far had 3 steals, as did Kawhi, helping contribute to the 12 steals and 6 blocks the Raptors had, outnumbering the turnovers they committed.

Despite their losing record, Toronto has shown a lot of growth this season. Without Kawhi in the lineup, they've given championship calibre teams a run for their money. Their youth has been a disadvantage, but the team sees all of these games as learning opportunities that they can look back on and use to get better.

The progress they've shown in the 12 games between their first matchup with Washington and now cannot be understated. Their shooting, while still not great, has shown improvement. Even if you only consider the last five games, all of which Toronto dropped (and three of those they played without Scottie), they had fewer turnovers, better shooting splits, and more assists in all of those games.

They're moving the ball better, playing as a team better, and shooting better. Of course Washington has had an opportunity to get better too, but this should be a good litmus test to see how the Raptors have grown so far this season.

The last time around, neither Anthony Davis nor Trae Young had particularly strong performances. Young shot 33% from the field and turned the ball over four times. Davis only managed nine points, by far his worst performance of the season thus far. While uncharacteristic for them, Toronto will need to force similar performances for them again. Murray-Boyles has a great opportunity here to shut Davis down and Shead could be a pesky and intriguing pairing against Young to slow him down, although Toronto's defence is best when they are working as a unit.

With any luck, they'll be able to move back into the win column, with a little boost in morale before they head to Detroit for another tough matchup.

Toronto: Scottie Barnes, RJ Barrett, Immanuel Quickley, Collin Murray-Boyles, Ja'Kobe Walter

Washington: Anthony Davis, Alex Sarr, Kyshawn George, AJ Dybantsa, Trae Young

Toronto: Kawhi Leonard, Nate Bittle, Kevin McCullar Jr.

Washington: Reece Beekman, Justin Champagnie, Felix Okpara, Tristan Vukcevic, Jamir Watkins

#2869. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2863. What we learned from the Spurs’ loss to the Pistons

If you were to design the perfect game plan to defeat the San Antonio Spurs, it would probably look a lot like what J.B. Bicketstaff drew up for the Detroit Pistons. San Antonio was picked apart and played right into the Pistons' hands in a 105-93 loss that snapped the Spurs' 12-game winning streak.

San Antonio jumped out to a 14-point lead thanks in part to Victor Wembanyama playing like an alien inside, tossing the ball off the backboard and dunking all over the Pistons. Then, once the otherworldly display ended, the Pistons' game plan took hold.

On defense, the Pistons packed the paint, overplaying Wembanyama's dives to the basket, playing him incredibly physically. They dared the Spurs' guards to take threes. San Antonio obliged, throwing up 40 three-pointers, making only 9 (22%). Detroit's pressure sped up San Antonio, threw off their offensive rhythm, and led to 15 Spurs turnovers.

Offensively, the Pistons countered San Antonio's strategy of putting Wembanyama on Ausar Thompson so he could roam the paint and protect the rim. They turned Thompson into a playmaking hub, using him in perimeter dribble handoffs. So when Wembanyama sagged off, the Pistons got easy mid-range jumpers. When he played up, Detroit found cutters in the paint. The Pistons outscored the Spurs 60-44 in the paint.

This is the game plan that good teams will deploy against San Antonio. Pack the paint, don't let Wembanyama get lobs, and force the three-guard lineup to hit threes. The Spurs have to find a counter to this strategy. Teams will implement it as long as Wembanyama dominates the paint.

Wembanyama shouldn't be exempt from criticism in the loss. Yes, he led the team with 25 points and 18 rebounds, but he only scored 8 points in the second half. That's because he settled for difficult jumpers instead of getting into the paint as he did in the first half. Wembanyama was taking what the defense gave him, but they were giving it to him for a reason. He is letting teams off the hook when he takes deep threes or pull-ups. The Spurs need more ways to get him the ball inside the arc with room to score.

One way Wembanyama has improved his scoring this year is in the post. His baseline turnaround jump shot has become one of his go-to moves. It's been falling, but he only took one of them against Detroit.

The bench unit continued to struggle. The scoring falls off a cliff when Wembanyama goes to the bench for a lot of reasons. San Antonio insists on playing Dylan Harper and Stephon Castle together, letting Fox go to the bench. Those two do not playmake at the same level as Fox, so it ends up turning into the guards going one-on-one, with Luke Kornet playing essentially no offensive role. If Fox were to stay in alongside one of Castle or Harper, at least the Spurs would have a true point guard who could set up one-dimensional offensive players like Kornet and Julian Champagnie.

Defensively, the units with Kornet and Keldon Johnson have struggled. That two-man lineup has a defensive rating of 116.8 and a net rating of -17.1

Devin Vassell's shooting slump has hurt San Antonio. The three-guard lineup only works if he can make defenses pay for packing the paint. Vassell went 2-for-6 from three on Tuesday night and is now shooting 31.4% from deep this season.

Harper left the game with what seemed like a non-contact groin injury. If it's serious, that could mean the guard will miss at least a few weeks, at most a month and some change. That's a big problem for San Antonio, which has relied on Harper's scoring particularly in close games. Expect a combination of Jordan McLaughlin, David Jones-Garcia, and the rookie Ja'Kobi Gillespie to fill in. My two cents: give the rookie a chance. Gillespie would give them some much-needed pull-up shooting in bench lineups.

#2861. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2858. The Columbus Blue Jackets Are Slumping, But Treating Adam Fantilli As A Scapegoat Is The Wrong Thing To Do

Last night, in a 5-1 loss to the New York Islanders, Columbus Blue Jackets forward Adam Fantilli was seemingly benched.

He played a total of 12:59, and only 3:35 in the third period. He went 6/9 on his faceoffs and was a minus-3. He also had zero shots on goal for just the second time this season.

Don Granato said of Adam Fantilli's limited usage last night, "You manage your bench with decision-making in mind, right? We had 11 forwards. If there are guys that are going better and guys that are impacting the game in different ways, yes, we're making decisions based on that."

That's all fine and dandy, but you have to just let this team play at this point. You're in the middle of a serious slump, and young guys can't be looked at as scapegoats.

In Basketball, at pretty much all levels, when a kid is in a shooting slump, the coaches say, "Shoot your way out of it." And guess what? It usually fixes itself if they keep shooting.

Adam Fantilli is second on the team in shots with 53, behind Zach Werenski. He's only had two games in which he's had zero shots. Kent Johnson has had three games with zero shots, while Werenski has had shots in every game.

Fantilli is also leading the team in faceoff win percentage at 60.9%. He's third on the team in hits and takeaways, and second on the team in total shots attempted. In terms of fancy stats, He's 4th on Corsi and 8th in Fenwick. It's his 9.4 shooting % that's way down from his previous seasons, and that seems to be the issue for him right now.

The entire team minus Werenski and Johnson could be put on this same boat.

Let the kid shoot his way out of the slump he's in instead of benching him. It does no good to have him on the bench. Yes, the team is bad right now, but benching someone doesn't suddenly "Wake the rest of the team up."

It's going to take a lot more than a single guy breaking out of a scoring slump to get this team on the right track.

Next Up For Columbus: The Blue Jackets are back on the road to play the Chicago Blackhawks on Thursday at 8 PM.

#2857. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations

#2854. U.S. soldier taken into military custody over suspected spying for Russia

LONDON — The American military has taken a U.S. Army soldier into custody after he was arrested in the U.K. on suspicion of spying for Russia, British authorities said Tuesday evening.

Senior serviceman Lt. Col. Brandon Carroll Tyner was arrested in England last week as part of an investigation by Britain's Counter Terrorism Policing with assistance from U.S. authorities, including the military and the FBI.

He was assigned to the NATO military alliance's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps (ARRC) and stationed at its headquarters in the county of Gloucestershire, working in human resources.

ARRC has more than 400 permanent staff from more than 20 nations ready to deploy land forces where they are needed.

The U.S. Army confirmed Tyner's identity to CNBC on Wednesday.

Following his arrest, the 41-year-old was detained in London on suspicion of committing offenses under Sections 1 and 3 of the U.K.'s National Security Act.

Authorities did not share details of the case, but Section 1 offenses relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, while Section 3 covers assisting a foreign intelligence service.

CNBC reached out to the Russian government for comment on the latest developments in the case. Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told reporters on Nov. 11 that Moscow had "absolutely nothing to do with" the situation, following the soldier's arrest.

British prosecutors engaged with the U.K. Attorney General's Office and American partners after what U.K. officials called "an extensive investigation."

A decision was then made to pass the investigation to U.S. authorities, in line with international protocol.

FBI Director Kash Patel said in a statement on Tuesday that the service has "zero tolerance for anyone who betrays their oath to the United States or puts our national security at risk."

U.S. Brig. Gen. Sean Stinchon said in a statement that the case should serve as a "stark warning to any service member suspected of violating the oath we all take to protect and defend the Constitution."

"Whenever we suspect somebody of betraying the trust of their fellow soldiers and jeopardizing our collective defense, we will identify you, and we will ensure justice is served," he said.

"These are extremely serious allegations, and our collective response should send a strong message that we will act swiftly and robustly where we suspect this kind of espionage activity taking place," Laurence Taylor, head of the U.K.'s Counter Terrorism Policing, said as the updates on the case were announced.

"We will continue to support our U.S. colleagues as they take the matter forwards," he added.

#2850. Knicks Bulletin: ‘The cardiac Knicks find a way’

The same we thought we'd never see GTA VI hit the proverbial shelves…

…I'm sure there'll be one day the Raptors beat New York again. Not yesterday, though.

Here's a bunch of quotes and comments coming off another Knicks dub in Toronto.

On Darko Rajaković's coaching and the Raptors' defensive intensity:

"Darko does a heckuva job… those guys play hard, really good defensively, give us fits."

On Jalen Brunson's late four-point play creating the separation needed to beat Toronto:

"That created the separation that we needed to be able to put them away. So just a big shot. It was one of those things where the play broke down, and we kept looking for an opportunity while spacing the floor. And Jalen found one and let it fly."

On the Knicks' late-game execution and contributions from Towns, Anunoby, Bridges and Shamet:

"I thought our execution, especially down the stretch, the last few minutes of the game, was at a pretty high level, on both ends of the floor, but particularly offensively. Our pacing, our spacing, our screening — we took care of the basketball down the stretch.

"We got a lot of different guys that produced in a lot of different ways tonight. Obviously, KAT continues to be a double-double machine. OG [Anunoby] was our defensive player of the game. … Mikal was good for us, especially down the stretch. And Landry Shamet was huge."

On his improved relationship with Karl-Anthony Towns and the importance of adapting his coaching philosophy:

"It's just part of being around each other. You hope your relationship with everybody grows. That's not gonna always happen but you hope it does. KAT's got a really good feel of how to play the game. Obviously he's a perennial All-Star. So those things you've always got to take into account when it comes to trying to listen or try to adapt more to what their style of play is.

"As a head coach — come in, it's your first year, and you have an idea what you want to do. Sometimes it doesn't fit. Offensively, or defensively. And that's when you've got to pivot. And so we kept pivoting and pivoting and building until we found a nice little groove. We feel pretty good about each other right now."

On recovering from the ankle injury scare after his four-point play:

"Adrenaline was pumping, and went back out there."

On Josh Hart and Mikal Bridges regaining confidence and contributing to the Knicks' success:

"This is a huge night for them, the way they played. They're gaining confidence every single night. They're going to continue to do great things for us and they're only going to get better."

On the Knicks rediscovering their ability to win close games:

"A lot of times when we get in games like that, especially last year, the cardiac Knicks find a way."

On gaining Mike Brown's trust and having more input in the team's strategy:

"It's more familiarity with the system and building that trust with Mike where he can trust me with things I see and say, which started in the playoffs. So building off that this year. Having more input and him hearing me out."

On acting as a second coach and collaborating with Brown during games:

"Him trusting me like a second coach. Me and him are always coaching with each other in the game, looking at game plans and plays and maybe ideologies and things I see that can help our team win, especially in close games. Working with him and coaching on the floor. It's been great. It's been fun."

On how his relationship with Brown has improved since the championship run:

"We just grew. We've grown with each other. And obviously there's trust that has to be established and both of us trust each other a lot. And the last playoff run showed it. So just building off that trust and the relationship that was built in the playoffs and having it make our team better this year."

On Jose Alvarado's professionalism and importance to the Knicks' locker room:

"Jose is a consummate professional. He's probably one of the best locker room guys we have, and one of the best people we have on the bench. His energy, just his presence, with a jersey on or a warmup on, it means more for this team than what the stats show."

"Has been kicking my ass for a couple weeks."

On stepping up his game on Tuesday after struggling through a few games:

"I think it was a step in the right direction. I think I'm obviously getting into the flow, into the rhythm of things, but today was a step in the right direction. Still a lot to improve on, but you got to start stacking days at some point."

On how his contract situation can amplify the pressure of poor performances:

"That's a low-hanging fruit. Sometimes some of those things amplify because you're in a contract year, essentially. It's human nature.

"It's in the back of your mind, and when you start focusing on those things, negative outcomes happen."

On trying to overcome his struggles and improve his mindset:

"When you're in the middle of it, there's not much that can really help. At the end of the day, you just have to go out there. And I think today I had a little bit of a better spirit."

On the negative thought patterns that have affected his performances:

"I'm struggling to shoot the ball, but it's one of those things where you start looking at the negatives and that's all you see. You don't see some of the positives. Then it's a snowball effect. One thing happens and you're trying to get yourself out of a hole that you're not giving yourself the ability to get out of. It's about playing with that joyful spirit, looking at things half full instead of half empty."

On dealing with physical and mental challenges simultaneously:

"For me, this (rough patch) is a little harder because I'm trying to get the mental aspect right and the physical aspect right. It can be easier when you're just dealing with one of those things. … I'm battling both of those things. I'm trying to give myself a little bit of grace with that."

On maintaining a positive attitude despite losing his place in the Knicks' rotation:

"I think it's more how I was raised [by] my parents. Treat people how you want to be treated. Sometimes the situation isn't going to be what you want. You can't control certain things and control what you can and that's your attitude, your energy every day. So I try to do that.

"I'm human, I have days where it's not going to be all lovey-dovey. But I always tell myself, 'Whether I'm not playing or not, I got the best seats in the house.' And I get to support my teammates. So I try to go with that mindset as best I can and my teammates allow me to be myself."

On the viral clip showing him comforting Mikal Bridges on the bench:

"[Mikal] is fine. We are all human. The camera caught us that time, but I probably do stuff like that 100 times during a game."

On staying prepared for opportunities after falling out of the rotation:

"The season is long. I've been on the other side. When I first got in the league [in 2021], I wasn't even touching the court. Then eventually I got to touch the court and get some reps in and played a huge role in the rotation.

"Sometimes you get out of it. The only way to get back is stay ready because an opportunity is going to come, you just don't know when. So that's the hardest thing is the guessing game of when that will happen. But you work, work and work and trust that it will come in the end of the day."

On how close the Raptors came to beating the Knicks in their previous meeting:

"After rewatching and rewatching and rewatching film, we played against the NBA champions and, in my three-and-a-half years here as coach, that's when we came the closest to beating them. We're getting better. Just one free throw and the game is completely different."

#2847. As Cowboys strip DC Christian Parker of play-calling, Brian Schottenheimer asserts stakes: 'If we don't make the playoffs, it's a failure'

In Houston and Denver, the decisions took just four games this season.

Head coaches DeMeco Ryans and Sean Payton stripped their defensive and offensive coordinator of play-calling responsibilities in early October. An 0-4 start for Houston and a 2-2 start in Denver convinced each man at the helm to send his unit in a different direction.

Against that backdrop, the Dallas Cowboys stripping defensive coordinator Christian Parker of play-calling this week seems less rash. But Parker was still just 10 games into his play-calling career when Dallas transferred that responsibility to senior defensive assistant Mike Pettine, whose experience includes eight seasons as a defensive coordinator and two as head coach.

Pettine will call defensive plays beginning this week against the Tennessee Titans, Cowboys head coach Brian Schottenheimer confirmed Wednesday.

"I plan on him being the play-caller for the year," Schottenheimer added.

Dallas' Christian Parker won't be making the primary defensive calls on gameday.

It's a drastic shift from the confidence and excitement that surrounded the 34-year-old Parker coming out of training camp, when a highly vocal and schematically multiple group of defenders had Dallas brass wondering whether they could solve multi-year defensive woes.

Parker has not fixed the Cowboys' defense as the unit ranks third-worst in points allowed per game (28.2) and worst in yards (393.8). But consider that last season's Cowboys ranked worst in points allowed and third-worst in yards a year after the Cowboys ranked second worst in points allowed and bottom-five in yards.

The issues predate Parker. The fix remains elusive.

Still, after ceding 46 points and a Cowboys home record-worst 584 yards to the San Francisco 49ers, Parker's role has been reduced. He will continue to run meetings and lead walkthroughs during the week even as his play-calling input will shrink to secondary.

Schottenheimer acknowledged that the urgency is high for the 4-6 group that holds just a 29% chance of making the playoffs, per Next Gen Stats projections.

"If we don't make the playoffs, it's a failure," Schottenheimer said. "The season's a failure. That's what we play for. We play to win and we play to make the playoffs, and we've got seven games left. "We need to put together a run of success."

In aggregate, the Cowboys have produced on offense. Their 28.6 points per game rank fifth-best in the league and their 368.3 yards rank eighth.

But Dallas has not beaten a team on track to make the playoffs.

The Cowboys' run game has jelled inconsistently, and their ability to finish games deserves questions on both sides of the ball.

Even as Dallas shifts from Parker to Pettine this week, early competition will only test improvement to a degree. The 1-8 Tennessee Titans visit AT&T Stadium this week, followed by the 2-7 Philadelphia Eagles on Thanksgiving.

Then Dallas' tougher stretch will come: the Seattle Seahawks before the Cowboys' bye, followed by the Los Angeles Rams, Jacksonville Jaguars and New York Giants.

How intentional was shifting defensive play-callers before a lighter schedule stretch?

"Not at all," Schottenheimer said. "Because again, we live in the moment. This decision was based off of the last few weeks and just looking at what was best for the team and not about who you're playing."

The last few weeks, for Dallas, have featured an overtime loss against the Arizona Cardinals despite a 14-point lead early in the fourth quarter followed by a game against the trade deadline-selling Indianapolis Colts that required a fourth-quarter comeback to eke out a win.

Technically, the Cowboys lost by only 6 points on Sunday to the 49ers. But San Francisco led 38-19 early in the fourth quarter and never trailed in the second half.

At least as damaging: The 49ers scored touchdowns on plays of 74, 70 and 66 yards.

The Cowboys have allowed nine plays of 50+ yards this season and 47 of 20+, per Pro Football Reference data.

Schottenheimer met with Parker on Monday afternoon to discuss the challenges.

"It was very honest," Schottenheimer said. "It was very forthright. We have that type of relationship that we can do that. And so, slept on it, picked [it] up again Tuesday morning, having really good conversations, talking about different things. And then the decision was made mid-morning on Tuesday."

Schottenheimer alerted the Cowboys' staff on Tuesday followed by his players on Wednesday. He continued to preach support of Parker even as he demoted him.

Does that mean Parker could get his play-calling job back?

"I mean again, my focus right now is just trying to beat the Titans," Schottenheimer said. "But like I said, I think CP has a very, very bright future as a coach, but more specifically a very bright future here as our defense coordinator. …

"He's a critical piece of what we're trying to do as we search for answers and solutions."

The remainder of the Cowboys' season will influence not only Parker's coaching future but also Schottenheimer's.

The Cowboys have not won consecutive games since last November. In two seasons, Schottenheimer has led the team to an 11-15-1 overall record.

An offense featuring Dak Prescott, CeeDee Lamb and George Pickens has flashed high ceilings but inconsistency. A defense that struggled under one-and-done coordinator Matt Eberflus last year has flailed for solutions.

Dallas shifted its defensive structure from a 4-3 to a 3-4 base for the first time in more than a decade this offseason. Personnel has turned over significantly, from trades for interior defensive lineman Quinnen Williams at last year's trade deadline and cornerback Joey Porter Jr. this October, to draft investments that included trading up for first-round safety Caleb Downs and doubling up in the first round with edge rusher Malachi Lawrence.

Missed tackles and communication errors are among the areas Schottenheimer said the team is emphasizing with the message of "doing simple better."

The Cowboys have allowed a league-high 1,350 yards after the catch, per Next Gen Stats. Pro Football Focus, an advanced site that does not know team assignments but does evaluate each team's game film, credits Dallas with 93 missed tackles through 10 games.

For context: PFF credits its top-graded tackling team this season, the Detroit Lions, with 52 missed tackles. PFF's lowest-graded tackling team, the Denver Broncos, have missed 123, per the site. The Cowboys rank 25th in tackling, per PFF.

Can Pettine, who hasn't called defensive plays since 2020, improve the unit?

Schottenheimer credited the 60-year-old as valuable for his perspective on how to use personnel and "really big on affecting the quarterback and pressuring the quarterback and he wants to get the offense unsettled."

Schottenheimer acknowledged his own accountability for the defensive woes, while saying he believes the staff change is a "delicate matter" but ultimately "fair."

When asked if Parker was akin to a young quarterback who could learn from a step back, Schottenheimer said he saw some merit in the comparison but "I don't think Christian Parker's being put on the bench."

Without improvement, Schottenheimer knows questions will grow about whether he will be.

"When you're in this situation and you're the head coach of a 4-6 football team, I mean, you're aware there's always going to be noise," Schottenheimer said. "But I don't focus on that. I really don't. I focus on trying to get these guys ready, trying to get them prepared, and that's my job and my sole responsibility."

#2846. Paris Hilton, Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez press House to pass Defiance Act to fight AI deepfake pornography

Artificial intelligence can turn an ordinary photo or video into a sexually explicit AI deepfake, with consequences that can follow victims into their classrooms and workplaces. Getting those images removed is one challenge. Holding the people who create or share them accountable is another. Policymakers, advocates and victims say additional federal legislation is needed, but a bill that would give victims new legal remedies has yet to receive a vote in the House after two years of efforts.

On Tuesday, Paris Hilton, one of the bill's most prominent advocates, was back on the steps of the U.S. Capitol alongside Defiance Act backers including Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez (D-NY) and Rep. Laurel Lee (R-FL) to press the U.S. House of Representatives to pass the bill into law. The U.S. Senate passed its most recent version of the measure by unanimous consent in January 2026.

The proposed bipartisan bill, the Defiance Act (Disrupt Explicit Forged Images And Non-Consensual Edits Act), would close gaps in an existing federal right to sue over sexually explicit deepfakes, creating the civil right to sue for victims of deepfake pornography. If passed, survivors could sue perpetrators who create, share, publish, solicit, or threaten to distribute this content without consent. Victims could also recover legal costs and seek court orders requiring the content to be removed.

"This work is very personal to me and it's just so terrifying now if AI gets into the wrong hands, what's possible," Hilton said during a new episode of the "CNBC Changemakers" podcast with Julia Boorstin.

When Hilton was 19, she was pressured into allowing an intimate recording featuring her and her then-boyfriend, Rick Salomon. She expected the recording to stay private and later found it was released without her consent.

AI has created an infinitely scalable platform for the online trauma she suffered.

"Somebody can literally just take an innocent photo and turn it into some explicit photo or video, and this is happening to millions of women and children all around the world, and they don't have anyone to protect them because the laws have just not caught up with this technology yet," Hilton said.

"There's girls who are teenagers in school. This is happening to them, and some of them are taking their own lives because it's just completely humiliated them, and destroyed their confidence ... being able to even walk into school," Hilton said.

Hilton is currently serving as chief advocacy officer at digital identity protection firm Loti AI, which helps people find unauthorized uses of their likeness or voice online and file takedown requests. She said the company has detected more than 100,000 explicit deepfakes depicting her and helped remove more than 60,000.

"I'm very fortunate that I have a team of lawyers. I have people helping me. But a lot of these young girls and women don't have that support," she said. "People need to know that there's going to be consequences for their actions," she added.

The Take It Down Act, which was signed by President Trump in May 2025, made certain disclosures of nonconsensual intimate images, including AI-generated deepfakes, a federal crime. Covered platforms must remove the material and any known identical copies within 48 hours of receiving a valid request.

Texas Sen. Ted Cruz introduced the bill in June 2024 after a case in Aledo, Texas, when an Instagram image of 14-year-old Elliston Berry was edited to make her appear nude and the altered image circulated on Snapchat. Berry told CNN that she woke up to calls and texts about the images. "It was out of my control. And they were spreading," she said. She also said it was difficult to tell her mother, even though she knew the images were fake.

As a mother, Hilton says she worries about what happens when these images circulate among classmates. "Even though the image is not real, the pain and suffering that comes along with it is very real," she said.

Before the Take It Down Act, state laws offered varying protections, and many did not explicitly cover synthetic images. That left victims with different options depending on where they lived, and, in some cases, reliant on platforms to act voluntarily. But Hilton says that current federal law sometimes "doesn't work," leaving victims in need of another way to hold perpetrators accountable. Other critics of the Take It Down Act's shortcomings have pointed to the reliance on federal prosecutors and lack of separate civil remedies for victims to pursue action.

The Sexual Violence Prevention Association, which helped author the Defiance Act, delivered letters to House Speaker Mike Johnson and House Majority Leader Steve Scalise on Tuesday, urging them to bring the bill to the House floor.

"I wrote the Defiance Act because survivors came to me with nowhere to go. Then, I was deepfaked by someone attempting to silence my advocacy for the Defiance Act. I was deepfaked while fighting for the very bill that would prevent it. Suddenly, I too had nowhere to go," said Omny Miranda Martone, founder and CEO of the SVPA, in a statement on Tuesday. "Now it's time for House leadership to say yes."

"The Defiance Act was designed to close those gaps. Once passed, it will help prevent digital sexual violence by giving survivors a way to hold perpetrators accountable and by ending the lack of consequences that emboldens them," Martone stated.

According to the SVPA, the Defiance Act has received 117 endorsements, including Microsoft and Snap, along with advocacy organizations such as National Organization for Women, Center for Democracy and Technology, and Center for AI Policy. In June 2026, OpenAI became the first AI company to endorse it, with Chris Lehane, the company's chief global affairs officer, calling it "smart legislation," and pushing for the House to pass it.

The Senate has passed versions of the bill twice, in 2024 and again in January 2026. Sens. Dick Durbin, the now-deceased Sen. Lindsey Graham (R-SC), Sen. Amy Klobuchar (D-Minn.) and Sen. Josh Hawley (R-MO) were among the original backers. Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez introduced the bipartisan House companion in May 2025, when it was referred to the House Judiciary Committee. It has never received a vote in the House.

In September, House Speaker Mike Johnson told reporters the bill was "good policy" but said the House had yet to build consensus around it. Some Republicans were reportedly reluctant to advance the measure this year because passage would hand Ocasio-Cortez a legislative win ahead of the midterm elections, according to a Politico report from July. Specific objections from House members have not been publicly shared.

The bill currently has 70 House cosponsors, evenly split between 35 Democrats and 35 Republicans.

Martone told CNBC in an interview that, post elections, the SVPA is hopeful there is no political "blockage" of the type that was discussed pre-November, "because we have not had any substantive blocks."

There was also the factor of the race for Republican leadership in the House in the next Congress, which was voted on Nov. 10 and is no longer causing any interference. "Nobody has said, 'I don't like this bill because of this reason.' We have not gotten any feedback like that. We're talking from big tech to representatives to civil society organizations. We've gotten widespread support and no indication of any sort of pressure in one direction. … It's very much increasing harm ... mostly to women and children, and that's just something we cannot wait around for," Martone, who uses the pronoun they, said.

That makes Martone confident the bill will pass before the end of this Congress. "It is right at the finish line," they said.

If the House doesn't act before the current Congress ends, supporters would have to reintroduce the bill in the next Congress. Its path in January will depend on the new makeup of the House and whether its Democratic leaders make the measure a priority. The Democrats are projected to hold a majority in the new Congress, though its exact size is not yet known.

Martone said "if absolutely necessary," the Defiance Act will be reintroduced next year, and Democratic leadership has already spoken with backers, "and they are championing the bill."

But Martone added that is not an ideal scenario. "If we wait for next Congress, we're going to get why we don't just wait for a new president. We can't keep telling survivors to wait until we have the perfect political conditions," they said.

#2845. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2844. The Hornets’ collapse against Lakers was so bizarre fans are crying conspiracy

The Charlotte Hornets were always going to be in for a strange season after selling low on LaMelo Ball over the summer. Training camp injuries to top players Kon Knueppel and Coby White put Charlotte in an even more precarious position entering the season, and it didn't take long for star wing Brandon Miller to suffer his own groin and hip strains. The Hornets knew they were taking a step back this year after trading Ball, but they still had a chance to earn a statement win on Tuesday night against the Lakers.

Instead, Charlotte's late-game choke job feels like a new low for this season, and had NBA fans looking for conspiracy theories to explain exactly what happened. The Lakers beat the Hornets, 118-114, in one of the strangest games of the 2026-27 campaign so far.

The Hornets led for most of the night, and built an 18-point lead at one point in the second half. It all came crashing down late in the fourth quarter after Miller turned the ball over and White missed a free throw, which allowed Quentin Grimes to splash a game-tying three and send the game to overtime. Things really started to get weird in the extra session.

The Hornets were down one with the ball with 18 seconds left. Charlotte head coach Charles Lee threw out a lineup that couldn't even get the ball in. Christian Anderson, Sion James, Royce O'Neale, Liam McNeeley, and Grant Williams took the floor for the most important possession of the game, with White, Miller, and Naz Reid nowhere to be seen. Grimes broke up the pass, earned the Lakers the possession, and eventually the win.

Lee said after the game that White and Miller were on a minutes restriction, which prevented them from playing the last 18 seconds.

"As they've been coming back from injury, we've been trying to build them up … you're always tempted as competitor … but we have a big picture in mind and it's a long year."

What's especially odd is that McNeeley had not played a second of the game before that possession, while Anderson only played three minutes back in the third quarter.

The Hornets must really trust their team doctors to avoid playing White and Miller due to a minutes restriction when they really needed them. Lee has always been a tremendous head coach in my eyes, but this is an example of when he could have used another voice in the huddle to preach common sense.

It's not like the Hornets were trying to lose on purpose, because there's no value to tanking this year after the NBA's lottery reform rules when into place. That still didn't stop fans watching the game from trying to explain Lee's decision-making with gambling, analytics, or anything else.

This game had extra attention on it because it was broadcast nationally on Peacock/NBC, and because the late tip time made it an "island game" with no competition for night owls. The Hornets probably wish so many people weren't paying attention this morning. Charlotte has been terrible late in games all year, and this is just another example.

Letting Quentin Grimes score 47 points on you is humiliating enough. Losing a game like that is even worse.
