#2764. I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

SHENZHEN, China — It took a trip to China for me, an American, to see U.S. President Donald Trump in person for the first time.

Trump emerged from a group of black-clad photographers and other personnel surrounding him at a welcome banquet in Shenzhen for the Asia-Pacific Economic Leaders' Meeting held Nov. 17 to 19.

Seated just a few spots to his left was Russian President Vladimir Putin who picked up a glass of red wine to join a toast led by Chinese President Xi Jinping.

"At tomorrow's Leaders' Meeting, we will discuss measures to expand mutual opening up, explore pathways to innovation-driven growth, and identify opportunities to expand practical cooperation across various fields, so as to translate more initiatives into tangible results," Xi said in Mandarin, according to a CNBC translation.

Xi was speaking broadly, but Thursday's agenda includes a bilateral meeting with Trump, and their highly anticipated trilateral meeting with Putin.

But looking at the mood in the room, it seemed that their trilateral conversation had already started Wednesday night at their long banquet table. Much of what happens at APEC, including bilateral meetings and other exchanges between leaders, takes place away from the media.

But what struck me was how little sign there was of the tension surrounding Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang resignation, a controversy that has gripped markets for the past 10 days.

Ahead of Trump's entrance into the banquet hall, Chinese Commerce Minister Wang Wentao shared a friendly moment with U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and other members of the American delegation.

Last week, the U.S. officials said that Trump did not plan to raise allegations of Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy during his meeting with Xi.

It was a striking contrast to just two months ago, when I saw photographs of Huang and Tesla CEO Elon Musk sharing a table with Trump and Xi in Washington, D.C.

And when Trump visited Beijing in May, Huang took a few minutes to speak with reporters, including me, inside the Great Hall of the People. Musk was there too, accompanied by one of his sons.

This time, neither were visible in a room that was closely watched by the world. Musk was expected to join Trump's delegation in Shenzhen, but I did not see him Wednesday evening.

Their absence from the government-heavy banquet was striking. The world's most powerful leaders may be grappling with the implications of artificial intelligence, but they certainly didn't need the industry's biggest names at the table to do so.

Trump arrives in China for historic meeting with Xi, Putin

I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

Why the Middle East is watching the Trump-Xi-Putin summit

China's Xi calls for removing trade and investment barriers

Putin says EU is losing industry, falling behind competition

Three things to watch as Trump, Xi and Putin meet in China

Over the months I've spent following APEC's "China Year," one thing has become clear: The forum has given Beijing an opportunity to host the U.S., Japan and other member economies at a time when unresolved tensions might otherwise have made such gatherings difficult.

But even in a year defined by talks of U.S.-China "strategic stability," the APEC events have exposed the rifts and the shifting dynamics in global power. Those dynamics were on display for the opening of the APEC CEO Summit.

Several hundred attendees, from Xiaomi CEO Lei Jun to U.S. Ambassador to China David Perdue, arrived early for the opening of the APEC CEO Summit.

After waiting in silence for at least 10 minutes, the audience welcomed Xi in a distinctly modern way: by raising their smartphones to capture the moment. The applause was rather muted.

It was much the same at the more government-heavy welcome banquet that evening. So many attendees rushed to take their own photographs of Xi and other leaders entering the room that reporters' views were largely blocked.

Following Xi's morning speech, there was a lengthy intermission before Malaysian Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim took the stage.

"The entire multilateral framework needs to change," he said, praising China on its "rare" recognition of smaller countries.

During the tea break that followed, attendees quickly resumed mingling. Some even brought coffee and snacks back into the room as Putin prepared to speak.

They listened as the Russian president talked about his country's economic resilience and claimed Europe was losing ground. As attendees headed to lunch, one thing stood out: Neither Trump nor any other G7 leader had taken the stage at the opening event. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney spoke later in the afternoon.

The most visible American presence during the morning session came from business leaders appearing in a promotional video ahead of Xi's speech.

And the absence of an American voice was perhaps even more noticeable at Wednesday evening's banquet.

Trump did not follow Xi with a toast of his own. This was the same famously outspoken president, who in a winding speech in Davos, had declared that the U.S. must get Greenland.

Yet on Wednesday night in China, Trump wasn't the one holding the microphone.

#2763. Giants’ Cam Skattebo makes abrupt backflip declaration: ‘I’m serious’

Giants star running back Cam Skattebo insisted Wednesday that he would no longer perform his signature backflip celebration during games.

His decision came in the wake of Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson tearing his ACL while doing a simple leaping sack celebration on Sunday.

During Thursday night's loss to the Commanders, three Giants players — Skattebo and wide receivers Malachi Fields and Darnell Mooney — all did backflips in the end zone after Skattebo scored a touchdown.

Fields said Wednesday that Giants coach John Harbaugh did not address the team about Hutchinson's injury. So Harbaugh didn't tell Skattebo or anybody else to avoid a backflip celebration in the future.

Skattebo reached the decision on his own.

"I'm done flipping, just for that reason," Skattebo said of Hutchinson's injury. "It's hard to see. Don't want it to happen to me."

"I'm serious," he said when pressed. "There's no reason to flip. Mentally, I'm like: 'F--- it. I'm not doing that s---.'"

He added, regarding Hutchinson's injury, "That s--- ain't going to happen to me."

Skattebo meant he would avoid that sort of unforced season-ending injury by no longer doing his backflip celebration.

Still, at one point during a brief interview on Wednesday, Skattebo left the door slightly ajar to his back flip returning.

"I'm sure I'll do it at some point, if we go to the playoffs or something," he said.

Before Skattebo granted an interview on the matter, he chimed in while Fields was discussing Hutchinson's injury.

"Just bad luck, man," Skattebo said from his nearby locker. "Just bad luck."

Skattebo suffered a gruesome season-ending ankle injury last year, but that didn't happen during a backflip situation.

This past offseason, at a fan event in Manhattan, Skattebo took a spill while attempting a backflip on a stage. He was not injured.

After that incident, Harbaugh essentially told Skattebo to stop doing backflips in off-field or non-game situations.

Skattebo said during training camp that he would avoid doing backflips in any non-game settings, including practices. He was noncommittal about whether the touchdown celebration would return during games in 2026. It has.

Giants think underwhelming high pick has 'turned the corner,' and could be re-signedNov. 17, 2026, 3:34 p.m.

Skattebo has four touchdowns, all rushing, as the 5-3-1 Giants prepare for Sunday's massive home game against the Jaguars.

It sounds like his fifth touchdown of the season, whenever it comes, will not be punctuated by his familiar flip.

#2762. Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other headliner in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

"He got the German mustache to go along with that right now, too, so he's hitting all themes there over in Germany," Florio said.

"You've gotta be careful trimming that thing. If you're in Germany, you've gotta be careful trimming that thing," Simms responded.

An off-mic producer was apparently thrown off by the comments, too, and Florio told him to lighten up.

Mike Vrabel and the Patriots fell to the Detroit Lions in Germany this past weekend.

Florio has been a staple in NFL media for decades now, and his site, Pro Football Talk, partnered with NBC back in 2009. Simms was a quarterback in the league for five years himself, most recently in 2009, and he joined NBC Sports in 2016.

Vrabel and the Patriots sit at 5-5 on the season after Sunday's loss, which marked their second straight. The Patriots declined to comment to Front Office Sports about the exchange. Patriots owner Robert Kraft is Jewish, and has long led efforts against antisemitism.

#2760. Retail investor enthusiasm for Anthropic IPO to rival SpaceX debut

Retail investor Dan Shetty is so eager to get in on the Anthropic initial public offering that he "drained" his Roth IRA and moved roughly $12,000 from Fidelity to Robinhood for a chance to participate.

"I don't want to tell my grandkids that I didn't invest in Anthropic and I didn't invest in OpenAI during the AI bubble," he said.

Shetty's enthusiasm is shared by many, with excitement among small investors for the blockbuster IPO expected to be off the charts. Vanda Research estimates that Anthropic could see approximately $250 million of retail net buying when it starts trading Thursday, double the size of SpaceX's IPO.

"That would be far and away the largest amount of net retail buying of any stock that we've seen in our data on the first day of its listing," Jai Malhi, senior strategist at Vanda, said in an interview with CNBC.

The artificial intelligence laboratory — which will trade on the Nasdaq under ticker symbol ANT — is selling 825 million shares at an expected price range of $70 to $78 per share in the IPO, though sources told CNBC the stock is likely to price above that range. Individual investors are expected to be allocated as much as 20% in that initial offering. And if Vanda's figures prove correct, the buying enthusiasm will be big as regular trading begins.

Founded less than six years ago, Anthropic aims to raise as much as $64.4 billion, giving it a valuation of $1.405 trillion. If demand is especially strong, Anthropic could even raise more than the record $85.7 billion that SpaceX sold in its record IPO in June.

The tech trade has been in turmoil since Jensen Huang's departure last week as Nvidia CEO raised questions about the future of AI financing. Before then, a potential slowdown in data center development resulting from Democratic victories in the 2026 midterm elections, and signs of moderation in the capital expenditures boom, had already spooked investors.

These all stand in marked contrast to the market backdrop in place at the time of the SpaceX IPO. In June, both positioning and sentiment were high, Malhi said. The Anthropic IPO could see a different dynamic, however. Demand could be strong across the entire investor spectrum given that sentiment is depressed, meaning that the bar to beat expectations is "much easier to overcome," he explained.

"What we're seeing across both retail and institutional positioning is that there is plenty of dry powder to take part in this IPO," the strategist said.

For the 27-year-old New Yorker Shetty, who himself works at agentic data platform Data Workers, this is his first time vying to take part in an IPO. He said Anthropic's emphasis on "safety first" drew his interest.

"I've been telling all of my friends, 'If you have like $5, you should invest it in this stock because this is the pure-play AI leader,'" Shetty said. "I think of my father who invested in Microsoft during the dotcom bubble, and he divested from that stock, and it was such a silly thing to do."

Joe Pak, a former macro portfolio manager who is now an independent researcher outside London, is opting against participating in the Anthropic IPO for two reasons: the likelihood of limited allocation and current macroeconomic conditions.

He believes the odds are probably stacked against retail investors when it comes to the allocation of shares.

"It's very difficult as a retail investor to actually get an allocation in these blockbuster IPOs," the 36-year-old said. "You get maybe one share, and the base case is you don't get any at all. So, it's a bit of a catch-22. If you do get a lot of shares, it probably means that the institutional bid was weaker, and in that case, you don't actually want the shares at that level."

In terms of the economy, conditions "need to be favorable" to hold a position in a stock like Anthropic, he said. But with the Federal Reserve having raised interest rates in September and widely expected to do so again next month, the backdrop could be darkening.

"The risk that you have is the financial conditions deteriorating" before the company starts to grow into anticipated profit and revenue, which would justify purchase of the stock, Pak said.

While Pak is steering clear of the IPO, he did say that at a certain level, it would make sense to get into Anthropic.

Other retail investors such as Bryan Nearnberg share Pak's view.

"I'm planning to eventually buy Anthropic, just not at the IPO price, considering when the lockups expire and there's some selling pressure," said the 37-year-old Westchester County, N.Y. resident, who works in finance operations. "We are not at the end of this short-term negative headline sentiment."

IPOs are a sore spot for Nearnberg, who hasn't had much luck trading stocks on their opening day. His skepticism toward IPOs can be traced all the way back to when he was in college.

"I didn't get an allocation to the Facebook IPO, but I ended up buying it the first day of open. I got burned pretty quickly," Nearnberg told CNBC. "Same thing with Zynga. I didn't get an allocation, but [got] burned pretty quickly in terms of price."

As a result of those experiences, "I'm pretty sure we'll see a good entry price underneath the IPO for Anthropic by Memorial Day next year," Nearnberg said, citing sour sentiment toward technology and concern surrounding circular financing as short-term headwinds.

To be clear, all the retail investors CNBC spoke with are optimistic on AI as a transformative technology — even the ones sitting out the IPO. One trader said he believes in AI so much that he uses Anthropic's Claude, a conversational AI assistant, every day.

"From a price-to-sales ratio, Anthropic's like a really good deal," Faigen said. But "it's still a massive valuation for a $1.4 trillion company, and there are significantly better deals out there if you are a fundamental investor."

Faigen, who works for an agentic brokerage firm, said he's "leaning towards patience," as he believes Anthropic is going to be public "for a very long time."

"I just am not in a rush to get in on day one based off the valuation," he told CNBC.

Anthropic's valuation depends on its ability to sustain its rapid growth. In the nine months through Sept. 30, revenue reached more than $33 billion, 1,200% above the year-earlier period. Aggregate spending commitments total about $900 billion, primarily centered around computing power and infrastructure.

Breakneck growth leads plenty of retail and institutional investors to approach not just the Anthropic IPO but the entire AI trade with caution.

"A lot of it comes down to scrutiny over how much money they're spending on AI," said Bret Kenwell, U.S. investment analyst at eToro. "Investors want to see that those investments are turning into tangible returns, into tangible revenue."

— CNBC's Charlotte Morabito contributed to this report

#2759. Maikel Garcia-Bryce Miller trade gives fantasy rebound candidates new life

Reports emerged Tuesday that the Royals were considering trading Maikel Garcia for pitching help. The Mariners made those reports outdated quickly by meeting those demands.

Per ESPN's Jeff Passan, Seattle acquired Garcia on Wednesday along with relief pitchers James McArthur and L.P. Langevin in exchange for starting pitcher Bryce Miller, plus additional arms Logan Evans and Jose Ferrer.

The change of scenery spices up a pivotal 2027 for both headliners. Does this make either Garcia or Miller a better fantasy baseball pick for 2027 (assuming we have a season)?

MLB Free Agent Tracker 2026-27: Top players available, latest signings, and contract information

Stay up to date with the MLB free agent market this offseason, including player signings, contract details, and more as the 2026-27 Hot Stove gets underway.

After a breakout 2025 season (.286/.351/.449, 16 HR, 23 steals, 81 runs, 74 RBI) in which he received AL MVP votes, Garcia endured several stints on the sidelines.

A hand muscle strain that cost him more than a month during the summer, and he had other shorter bouts with elbow cramps, an early-season right hamstring strain, and a late-summer left hamstring strain.

That would explain his crater-like drop in stolen bases to just five in 2026. When you invest in Garcia, you want that security with stolen bases and batting-average base.

Sure, that statistic as a whole is volatile, but as Garcia's Baseball Savant/Statcast chart spells out, his combination of elite Chase/Whiff/Strikeout percentages point to one of the batters likeliest to have the fewest strikeouts in the majors.

In addition, his top-level glove work would keep him in the lineup so he could eventually fall upward toward 20 homers, just like what happened in 2025.

This may prove difficult, though. Garcia already sits near the bottom of annual lists in barrel rate, which doesn't predict heavy power growth without supremely good luck or a rapid bulk-up from his 6-foot-1, 195-pound frame.

Moving to T-Mobile Park, the Park Factor for right-handed hitters overall, for doubles, and for triples sits toward the bottom of the 29-park rankings, if not the bottom; homer ratings remained almost the same.

Much of Garcia's ability to regain his fantasy juice sits on how quickly he'll return to elite steal totals. Heading into his age-27 season, Garcia carries 2,174 plate appearances, more than what's typically needed before a significant breakthrough happens.

Plus, nestled in the middle of the Mariners order likely will serve him better than helping to carry a cooked Kansas City order that wilted aside from Bobby Witt.

His rebound potential and improved supporting cast likely will justify a return to the top-100 overall picks in fantasy drafts, with a significant positional advantage of a stolen-base upside play at third base.

Miller is still trying to round back into his peak form following a 2025 marred with bone spurs and inflammation in his right (throwing) elbow that helped lead to a 5.68 ERA, a far cry from 2.94 in his breakout 2024.

Despite missing time with an oblique strain, Miller looked like he was turning a corner in the first half last season; he held a 2.18 ERA at the All-Star break.

He took an ugly turn in the second half, though, dragging himself to a 6.05 ERA and a temporary move to the bullpen. Perhaps endurance was a challenge following his elbow concerns the year before.

Settling in at season's end with a palatable 4.18 ERA, many year-over-year improvements stand out, including:

Swinging Strike%: 10.6 to career-best 11.6

Perhaps a contributor to his first-half success was an offseason treatment that proved successful without surgery, according to MLB.com's Daniel Kramer's report from February:

"But the Mariners right-hander discovered a solution early in the offseason that allowed him to avoid undergoing a procedure that he'd long envisioned as inevitable.

The answer was a Synvisc injection, gleaned from consultations with renowned orthopedist Dr. Keith Meister and applied to improve joint lubrication, dampen inflammation -- and above all, relieve pain.

"After I got that, that's when I started feeling a lot better," Miller said. "I thought for sure I was going to have to get the spurs shaved down and removed."

Perhaps better management of the spurs will lead to more sustained results -- meaning Miller could once again deliver as an often fantasy ace throughout the season.

Jose A. Ferrer (4.18 ERA, 3 saves, 13 holds last year) should remain on watch lists for saves and holds leagues.

Logan Evans might eventually compete for a rotation spot with the Royals, but his middling stuff should limit fantasy interest to spot starts and AL-only leagues.

#2758. Barlow ‘making progress,' Embiid out vs. Pacers, more on Sixers

Barlow 'making progress,' Embiid out vs. Pacers, more on Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow decided to finish his post-practice workout Saturday with a windmill dunk.

While Barlow remains out with a "lower back stress reaction" listing on the Sixers' injury report, it's one sign that he's trending up in his rehab process.

"He's making progress," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He was a part of the practice today. It's kind of a game day type of practice … so it wasn't super intense, as you might expect. But he was out there doing some stuff. He's progressing. Just don't know when he'll be ready to go, but at least he's making some progress now."

Barlow was one of the Sixers' top role players last season, starting 59 games and contributing in a variety of areas. He averaged 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals in 23.8 minutes per game.

"Size, athleticism, rebounding," Dean Wade said of the fifth-year forward. "He's another guy that can switch one through five."

Whenever the 6-foot-9 Barlow is available for his season debut, the Sixers will welcome him back as another option at center.

With Ariel Hukporti's preseason Achilles tendon tear and Adem Bona's cervical spine sprain, the team is light on healthy resources at the five. And Joel Embiid (right foot plantar fascia injury management) will sit Sunday afternoon vs. the Pacers on the first leg of a back-to-back. He'll presumably return to the lineup when the Sixers visit the Nets on Monday night.

"I think (Barlow) is probably going to have to play more five than we envisioned," Nurse said, "but Jabari (Walker) is doing that as well. If the situation calls for that, I imagine that's where he'll help the most."

With the Delaware Blue Coats hosting the Maine Red Claws on Friday and Saturday, the Sixers saw an opportunity for two players outside of the regular rotation to log minutes with their G League affiliate.

Rookie guard Labaron Philon Jr. suited up Friday and third-year wing Justin Edwards played both games. Philon posted 20 points, nine rebounds and eight assists in a blowout loss. Edwards shot just 1 for 10 from the floor in the first game, but he scored 23 points on 9-for-17 shooting in Saturday's win.

Nurse has largely kept a tight rotation early in the season and the 10-3 Sixers haven't had many lopsided games with ample garbage time. As of Saturday night, the Sixers ranked 29th in the NBA in bench minutes per game.

"My first belief is it's hard to get better unless you're playing," Nurse said. "That kind of trumps everything, in my opinion. Just historically, we've had a number of guys that have gone down to those games, respected it … and it's really helped them in their careers. … It's been a minute for these guys — both of them — since they've had any real court time.

"So that fit into the schedule well. … I think it will also help them to just find some rhythm, find their legs a little bit. We're going to need everybody at some point, so keep 'em going."

VJ Edgecombe was among the crowd Friday night at Chase Fieldhouse.

"They're my brothers, man," he said. "Labaron, Justin, (Caleb Love), (Rayan Rupert). I just wanted to show love. … I didn't have (anything) to do yesterday. … We have that relationship off the court, too. I'm just being supportive and showing them that I care about them."

#2756. Big bets against AI debt, from CoreWeave to Oracle and the Mag 7, could soon hit the ETF market

Concerns about balance sheets and valuations in the artificial intelligence sector have hit the market hard since October, and investors who have shorted publicly traded tech companies in the AI trade have profited. Most investors don't think as much about "shorting" debt, but there will be a way through exchange-traded funds to profit from widening credit default swap spreads of top AI companies.

Both Tuttle Capital and Defiance ETFs have filed for funds that target the credit default swaps of companies closely linked to the AI trade, from CoreWeave and Oracle to the Magnificent Seven: Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms, Microsoft Corporation, Nvidia, Tesla, and Apple.

Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier. CDS is a closely watched indicator of issuer health in the debt markets, especially in the AI market this year. The ETFs are designed to gain when the CDS spreads of these companies widen, either as a group in the case of a Magnificent Seven CDS fund, or individually in the case of proposed ETFs targeting Oracle, CoreWeave and Meta. The funds would lose value when the market assesses the creditworthiness of these debt issuers as improving.

Both ETF managers filed for CoreWeave and Oracle CDS funds, specifically, among broader AI and semiconductor company CDS portfolios.

If the exchange-traded fund managers get approval from the Securities and Exchange Commission, these portfolios would be the first of their kind in an ETF market that has become more aggressive in recent years about launching higher-risk strategies revolving around more complex financial instruments than just public stocks and bonds. The ETF managers plan to employ leverage in the portfolios, which both increases risk and the potential for outsized returns on any given day, similar to the way existing single-stock leverage and inverse ETFs function.

The ETF managers say these CDS funds can serve an important role in a market on edge about AI debt.

"As AI infrastructure investment accelerates and companies take on significant capital commitments to fund that growth, we see an opportunity to give investors more targeted tools to manage credit risk and hedge potential financial stress across some of the biggest names driving the AI buildout," Sylvia Jablonski, CIO of Defiance ETFs, said in a statement to CNBC.

Neocloud companies like CoreWeave are a major focus for AI skeptics, an AI bubble watch compounded by the exit of Jensen Huang from Nvidia, who is seen as a critical player in financing the AI buildout, with Nvidia called by some the "central bank of AI."

Apollo Global Management CEO Marc Rowan on Tuesday said that neocloud company AI projects could face an existential crisis if Nvidia funding dries up. "There was a strong incentive for Nvidia as a business matter to get these projects off the ground," Rowan said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." "If that banking function, that guarantor function, gets taken away, access to capital for a large number of neoclouds is going to disappear."

In its earnings filings and conference call on Tuesday, Nvidia shifted the tone in discussing the way it finances the AI ecosystem.

Matthew Tuttle, founder and CEO of Tuttle Capital Management, said it is less likely that these ETFs will find an audience among retail investors than smaller institutional players.

"It may be a stretch for the average retail guy," he said, but he added that having managed leveraged and inverse ETFs, it is clear that many investors "get interested" when a particular asset is seeing outsized moves, as has been the case with AI company-linked CDS in 2026. "The stuff that moves a lot is the stuff that tends to generate the most interest," he said.

Both prospectuses stress that these funds are not designed to move inversely to the reference companies' stocks and can lose money while a company's shares decline. They also note these funds don't short bonds or bet directly against a company's stock, and to buy CDS protection, they pay premiums, which are a unique cost to the fund manager whether or not credit conditions worsen.

The CDS of these companies have been on the move in a major way as part of the AI trade over the past year.

CoreWeave's 9.75% bond due in 2031 is yielding close to 16%, while spreads on its five-year credit default swaps were recently between 1,100-1,200. Spreads over 1,000 are generally considered a sign of distress.

Oracle CDS contracts have moved up over the past year from below 80 to a current range above 300.

The CDS spreads on the biggest tech companies, including Amazon and Meta as examples, rose over 100% within the past year, as their free cash flow turned negative from spending heavily on AI. Even though the market is much less concerned about their balance sheets than that of the neoclouds, major tech company CDS spreads did hit five-year highs earlier this month.

Tuttle said that these ETFs, if approved, would be the "easiest" to use for smaller institutions and family offices that want CDS exposure and "are either too small to trade with the Goldmans of the world or lack the in-house expertise to trade CDS on their own."

"It is a different way to hedge the AI trade," Tuttle said.

ETF investing experts expressed skepticism about this approach and market reception. "I don't see much demand materializing for these types of products," said Nate Geraci, president of NovaDius Wealth Management. But he added it does make sense to target these ETFs "at retail investors who are seeing scary headlines around AI capex spending. Yet another way to speculate (gamble)," he said.

Todd Sohn, chief ETF strategist at Baird Strategas, said he foresees confusion with how these funds will work. "How many non-professionals know what CDS are?" he said.

Tuttle says it won't take billions in assets to make an ETF focused on AI CDS a minor financial success. "My criteria is I want to see a runway to $20 million, which is about our breakeven. I see it here. It doesn't mean it will happen, but I think we have a decent chance."

—CNBC's Michelle Fox contributed to this report

#2755. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2754. Vic Fangio sends vote of confidence to Cowboys’ Christian Parker after losing playcalling

Vic Fangio sends vote of confidence to Cowboys' Christian Parker after losing playcalling appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Dallas Cowboys defensive coordinator Christian Parker may have lost his playcalling duties, but not the confidence of at least one fellow defensive coordinator in him.

#2753. Fed officials expect more rate hikes, worry about AI impact on inflation, minutes show

Federal Reserve officials expressed confidence in October that further interest rate increases will be needed to stem inflation, though they decided to hold off while gathering more information, according to meeting minutes released Wednesday.

Policymakers put artificial intelligence at the core of their discussions, indicating that demand associated with the technology would be "a material source of inflation risk."

More broadly, officials said "inflation pressures in the economy were broad based" and would warrant action, with "some" policymakers advocating for a quarter percentage point hike at the meeting. A couple of participants even noted the possibility that a larger increase might be needed at the next policy meeting in December.

The meeting summary said "almost all participants judged that, under their baseline outlooks for inflation and the labor market, further action to raise the target range for the federal funds rate … would likely be appropriate, in order to ensure a timely return of inflation to the 2 percent objective."

In a CNBC interview Wednesday, Fed Governor Christopher Waller reiterated his belief that the Federal Open Market Committee should consider a half-point hike in December.

Officials at the meeting expressed a high level of concern over AI, both as a source of inflation and financial instability.

Fed staff members who briefed participants noted "that off-balance-sheet leasing commitments and circular financing arrangements among AI-related firms could amplify losses if returns on AI investment were to disappoint."

"Most participants observed that AI-related debt had grown rapidly and that much of this growth had taken place through complex and opaque financing structures," the minutes said. "A few participants remarked that AI-related exposures were becoming increasingly concentrated among a small number of firms."

Officials said they still expect inflation to fall off sharply in 2027 and beyond as the impact from the current shocks wear off.

Ultimately, the FOMC voted 10-2 to keep its benchmark overnight borrowing rate locked in a range between 3.75%-4%. Governor Lisa Cook and Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack dissented in favor of a quarter-point increase.

Those voting in favor of the hold "pointed to benefits in accumulating additional information, which would help in determining the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal."

Since the meeting, the inflation data has shown that the pace of price increases remains well above target.

The consumer price index in October rose 0.4% for all items and 0.3% for the core that excludes food and energy. Both readings were 0.1 percentage point higher than expected.

While indicating concern over how stubborn inflation has been, most Fed officials who have spoken publicly advocated a patient approach.

However, Waller said Wednesday that the Fed may have to "shock" markets by approving a half-point hike in December. He is the only central banker to espouse that belief publicly, though St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem said he anticipates as much as a full percentage point move higher by the spring of 2027. As governor, Waller is a permanent FOMC voter; Musalem will not vote until 2028.

Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee said Friday on CNBC that the central bank risks losing its credibility if it doesn't act.

At the core of policymakers' concern is whether rising prices related to the Iran war and President Donald Trump's tariffs, in many cases temporary events that wouldn't prompt a response, are instead becoming more entrenched.

Markets are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point hike at the Dec. 8-9 meeting that includes low odds of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch gauge. Futures traders also are pricing a solid likelihood of two more increases in 2027.

#2751. Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers

Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Kelly Oubre Jr. experience switched sides Sunday afternoon at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Back in Philadelphia with the visiting Pacers, Oubre was his usual self. There's never any risk of mistaking Oubre for somebody else.

"Love him," Pacers head coach Rick Carlisle said pregame. "Such an upbeat guy. He's such a unique person. Love the wardrobe, all the different stuff. He's a very authentically one-of-a-kind type guy."

Carlise then detailed Oubre's various basketball qualities, some of which he showed in Indiana's 131-124 loss. The 30-year-old lefty wing scored 16 points and grabbed four rebounds. His day also included a theatrical imitation of Jaylen Brown's off-arm push after the Sixers' star was whistled for an offensive foul.

Sixers fans grew familiiar with Oubre's strengths, weaknesses and eccentricities during his three seasons as a Sixer. He wound up becoming the team's third-longest tenured player behind only Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey. Oubre played 178 regular-season games and averaged 14.9 points, 5.4 rebounds, 1.6 assists and 1.3 steals.

After the Sixers replaced president of basketball operations Daryl Morey with Mike Gansey, Oubre signed a two-year contract with the Pacers in free agency.

"Mike came over from Cleveland and I heard whispers that he wanted some of his Cleveland guys," Oubre said before the game in the visitors' locker room. "And I was like, 'OK, cool. I'm a Philly guy. So I guess when he comes over, I will no longer be a Philly guy.' That was kind of the situation at hand.

"No bad blood because business is business at the end of the day. They have a great team over there, and they put together a team that they think is the best for them. I'll be all right elsewhere. I figured it out."

Oubre thought Indiana made sense as his next stop.

"It was the pace, the culture, the attitude that this team has," he said. "It's a fast-paced style of basketball. It's a bunch of guys who work collectively as a unit and kind of figure it out from there.

"I thought that would be a great opportunity for me to come and and kind of free-flow play, not have to think. I was wrong. Been thinking a lot, but I'm starting to figure it out and starting to get my rhythm here. … It's a great culture. Coach Rick Carlisle is a legend, Hall of Fame coach. And they wanted me. I love people who want me."

As Oubre settled into a new city, the Sixers had a stunning, transformational summer.

Gansey traded for Jaylen Brown, signed LeBron James, and added veteran role players like (former Cavalier) Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope. Oubre followed along.

"It was must-see TV, like (for) everybody else," he said. "As a basketball fan, it was pretty cool to see where I was kind of reinvent and get new life and new energy. So I was happy with that. Would I have loved to be a part of it? Of course.

"But at the end of the day, I'm a grown man and I've got to keep it moving and figure it out for myself. It was entertaining. … I'm kind of happy for all the people that have sacrificed over these years. … I'm trying to put a road block in that road for y'all."

Oubre joined the Sixers on a minimum-salary deal in September of 2023 after averaging 20.3 points the past season with the Hornets. Head coach Nick Nurse pitched Oubre on his potential to be a valuable, multi-dimensional player for good teams.

"I was literally about to be done with basketball," he said. "Coach Nurse called me multiple times and said, 'I want you to play for me.' And I was like, 'You know what? This guy really seems to care. He really wants me.' So thank you for just pulling me out of that pit and that hole that I was in four years ago. I got some new life in my basketball career.

"I'm always thankful for that and I love Nurse for that. Just appreciative for my time here. The battle wounds and scars that I've been able to build up, it made me a better man."

Throughout his Sixers stint, Oubre often spoke of growth, evolution, adaptation and self-discovery.

He talked again Sunday about his desire to "Be water" and how life as a Sixer changed him.

"I grew a lot, man," Oubre said. "Just the mental fortitude of being able to withstand adversity. Being able to wake up and not necessarily know which soliders are going to battle, just having to put on different hats on any given night. At first it was challenging, because I'm thinking about all the logistics and things and like, 'This is not what they asked me to do.' But at the end of the day, I just bought into being somebody that can be like water and grow with the team.

"It's been a blessing to be able to play with (VJ Edgecombe), be able to watch Tyrese (Maxey) ascend. Those are the guys where we were front line a lot. Just going through war with those guys was amazing. It just taught me mental fortitude. If I can make it through this, I can make it through anything."

Oubre felt he developed a meaningful connection with the Philadelphia fan base over his three years.

"When they talk about the City of Brotherly Love, I think they're talking about your soul, not necessarily about what you can offer them and what you do," he said. "Everybody in this city works hard. They wake up, go to work, keep the city going. And us as the prima donna basketball players, the least we can do is go hard and sacrifice for them so that they have something to cheer about when they come home from work.

"I think that was the relationship that we had. I'm forever indebted and forever grateful to the people in the city of Philadelphia."

#2750. CEO turned informant: How the FDA built a case against executives promising cheap medications

The federal case against an alleged illegal prescription drug operation and its two top leaders earlier this month features a notable informant: the CEO of a peer company that authorities say was committing the same import violations.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and director of a Canadian healthcare company called CANARX, was arrested in the tunnel connecting Detroit and Canada on Nov. 8 and charged with smuggling, conspiracy and importing a misbranded drug for patient use in the United States. His brother and the president of the company, Bob Howard, is wanted on similar charges and was still being sought as of last week, according to court records.

The charges mark the first criminal case by U.S. authorities against a company that's part of a cottage industry known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, that promise to lower or eliminate high prescription drug costs for Americans.

The cheaper medications are typically sourced from international markets, a business model that the Food and Drug Administration and Department of Homeland Security say is illegal and can put patients' health at risk.

At issue is the unregulated nature of drugs intended for foreign markets and, in some cases, the risk of counterfeit products. And while AFPs generally argue that the importation of medication is allowable under U.S. law by individuals for personal use, authorities contend the operations go too far.

CANARX, based in Windsor, Ontario, was established in 2002 and was featured in a CNBC investigation into the booming AFP industry.

Joseph Morris, general counsel for CANARX, told CNBC last year that the business was operating legally and was confident in the safety of the prescriptions it provided. Last week, Morris told CNBC that the Howard brothers "deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The FDA's case against CANARX started with a tip about a similar healthcare operation called ScriptSourcing. It is detailed in a 35-page affidavit — dated Oct. 29 and unsealed last week — written by Dustin Dobbs, special agent with the FDA's Office of Criminal Investigations.

Based in Bonita Springs, Florida, ScriptSourcing marketed its international prescription program to self-funded employer health plans. Founder and CEO Gary Becker was described on the company's website as a "health insurance and risk management consultant for more than 30 years."

ScriptSourcing had been on the FDA's radar since at least 2022 for potential drug import violations, according to the affidavit. In August 2025, Dobbs, posing as someone interested in the AFP business, emailed ScriptSourcing and asked if the company was FDA-compliant.

A ScriptSourcing employee in responses to Dobbs said the company was FDA-compliant and that it had more than 2,000 name brand specialty medications and worked with more than 3,000 self-funded health plans.

However, that same year, the agency learned of two alleged incidents in which medications from ScriptSourcing's international drug import program had harmed patients, according to Dobbs' affidavit. One patient had been hospitalized due to a small bowel obstruction after she used misbranded Mounjaro sourced by the company via a Canadian pharmacy, and another was allegedly seriously injured after receiving a counterfeit medication.

Facing scrutiny from the FDA, ScriptSourcing CEO Becker turned on the Howard brothers of CANARX.

By May 2026, Becker had become a cooperating witness for the government in its investigation into the Canadian firm, Dobbs wrote. Becker told the FDA that he had done business with CANARX on drug importation and that the company had given him a "road map" on how to operate, according to the affidavit.

ScriptSourcing provided the FDA with a spreadsheet of financial transactions with CANARX between 2021 and 2026. It showed approximately 16,549 prescription drugs have been dispensed, the affidavit said.

Then, in several recorded phone calls between June and July 2026, Becker discussed the foreign importations with the Howard brothers.

The phone calls "show that John Howard was aware of the routes for importation of drugs from Canada into the United States and has tried to mold them to fit [CANARX's] illegal drug importation program," Dobbs wrote in the affidavit. "These phone calls also show that John Howard and Bob Howard were aware that these drugs were not allowed to enter into the United States legally."

Among the shipments en route or being prepared by CANARX at the time the information was provided was a package that contained Neupro, an FDA-regulated drug for the treatment of Parkinson's disease and restless legs syndrome.

The FDA did not respond to a request for comment from CNBC on the details in the affidavit.

Gary Becker's attorney, George Karavetsos, declined to comment when reached by CNBC, but confirmed on Monday that ScriptSourcing was no longer in business.

The AFP business model was at the center of a civil lawsuit originally filed by Gilead Sciences in December 2024 that alleged several companies, including CANARX, illegally imported its HIV drug Biktarvy. CANARX has not responded to Gilead's allegations.

Gilead said in a statement to CNBC last week in response to charges against the Howard brothers that it "welcomed the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

"Pharmaceuticals manufactured for foreign markets are almost always considered unapproved and misbranded drugs under U.S. law, whether counterfeit or even if made by the same manufacturer that produced the version sold in the United States," Dobbs wrote in his affidavit.

— CNBC's Melissa Lee and Paige Tortorelli contributed to this report.

#2749. Twins Stun MLB With Sonny Gray Decision

NEW YORK, NEW YORK - SEPTEMBER 30: Sonny Gray #54 of the Boston Red Sox pitches against during the first inning in game two of the American League Wild Card Series at Yankee Stadium on September 30, 2026 in the Bronx borough of New York City. (Photo by Elsa/Getty Images)

BOSTON, MASSACHUSETTS - SEPTEMBER 11: Sonny Gray #54 of Boston Red Sox pitches during the top of the first inning of a game against the Kansas City Royals at Fenway Park on September 11, 2026 in Boston, Massachusetts. (Photo by Edward Monigan IV/Getty Images)

The Minnesota Twins didn't even let someone else break the news. While this is normally a time where ESPN's Jeff Passan has the information before anyone else, this time it was the Twins' social media team sharing one of the most surprising signings that will happen this offseason.

Sonny Gray has signed in free agency with the Twins. Yes, one of the top pitchers on the market, a guy who has pitched for the Boston Red Sox and New York Yankees among other stops, is heading to Minneapolis to continue his career.

The Twins posted the news a bit after 2:30 p.m. local time in Minnesota.

While the Twins have been floated as a possible destination in various free agent pursuits, this one didn't seem to actually be a likely outcome until right now — it has happened.

GettyNEW YORK, NEW YORK – SEPTEMBER 30: Sonny Gray #54 of the Boston Red Sox pitches against during the first inning in game two of the American League Wild Card Series at Yankee Stadium on September 30, 2026 in the Bronx borough of New York City. (Photo by Elsa/Getty Images)

Passan did eventually chime in. He shared the contract details a few minutes after the Twins signed Gray.

"Right-hander Sonny Gray and the Minnesota Twins are in agreement on a two-year, $55 million contract that includes an opt-out after the first season, sources tell ESPN."

Without having any real insights beyond this yet, that does seem like a higher dollar figure than some other teams would've offered Gray.

The two-year deal with the opt-out is also ideal. If Gray pitches well, he can pursue a new contract again next offseason. If he struggles or gets hurt, Gray has another payday awaiting him in 2028.

GettyBOSTON, MASSACHUSETTS – SEPTEMBER 11: Sonny Gray #54 of Boston Red Sox pitches during the top of the first inning of a game against the Kansas City Royals at Fenway Park on September 11, 2026 in Boston, Massachusetts. (Photo by Edward Monigan IV/Getty Images)

Gray, who just turned 37 earlier in November, just completed a fantastic season with the Red Sox.

He went 18-5 in 29 starts with a 2.72 ERA. Gray pitched 169 innings and had 149 strikeouts.

Gray has a career 3.51 ERA at the MLB level 143 career wins.

He has pitched for the A's, Reds, Cardinals, Yankees, Red Sox — and the Twins before.

Gray was with the Twins in 2022 and 2023, going 16-13 across the two years with a 2.90 ERA.

The fact that this is a return for Gray also likely helped close the deal.

#2748. Rams’ Kyren Williams, Blake Corum keep team afloat during rough waters

The Rams have spent millions assembling a roster loaded with superstars, former MVPs and future Hall of Famers. Yet two of their most valuable offensive weapons continue to operate without nearly the recognition they deserve.

Kyren Williams and Blake Corum don't have the MVP trophy of Matthew Stafford, the celebrity status of Myles Garrett, the highlight-reel of Puka Nacua, the television commercials of Davante Adams or the Hall of Fame résumé of Aaron Donald.

But what they do have is production. And lately, they've been carrying the Rams' offense on their backs like a couple of firefighters hauling people out of a burning building.

Los Angeles Rams running back Blake Corum (24) leaps past Los Angeles Chargers cornerback Elijah Molden (2) on his way to scoring a touchdown during the second half of an NFL football game. AP Photo/Caroline Brehman

In doing so, they've rescued the Rams' season, and a new social media analysis suggests NFL fans are finally beginning to notice.

According to data compiled by BetOnline Sports & Casino through the first 10 weeks of the NFL season, Williams ranks third among the league's most underrated players based on fan conversations on X. Corum also appears among the players drawing attention for being overlooked.

It's hardly a scientific measure of talent. But the numbers on the football field make a much stronger argument.

Williams leads the NFL in rushing success rate for the second consecutive season, converting 61.4% of his carries into successful plays.

Corum, meanwhile, leads the league with 0.17 expected points added per rushing attempt, a measure of how much each carry improves his team's chances of scoring.

Among running backs who recorded at least 100 carries and averaged eight attempts per game in 2025, Corum ranked fifth in yards per carry and second in rushing success rate, trailing only Williams.

Corum also is top 10 in missed tackles, gains of 15 yards or more, and is one of the NFL's most efficient running backs. Oh, and he's only fumbled twice in two years.

Think about that. Two running backs sharing the same backfield finished first and second in a category measuring how consistently they keep an offense moving down the field.

And somehow, they're still being overlooked.

Corum's emergence has given coach Sean McVay the kind of reliable one-two punch the Rams haven't enjoyed in years. Williams brings toughness, receiving ability and dependable pass protection. Corum adds patience, vision and the explosiveness to turn a modest opening into a significant gain. And neither needs 25 carries to make an impact on the game.

Their value became impossible to ignore after injuries stripped the Rams of their top receiving weapons for Sunday's rematch in the desert with the Cardinals.

Blake Corum #22, Kyren Williams #23, and Ronnie Rivers #20 of the Los Angeles Rams celebrate. Getty Images

With Nacua and Adams sidelined, the Rams needed somebody to keep their offense functioning. Williams and Corum answered the bell.

Corum rushed for 100 yards and a touchdown on 17 carries, averaging 5.9 yards per attempt. He added three receptions for 19 yards as the Rams won their fourth consecutive game and improved to 6-4.

McVay even proved why many across the league see him as an innovative offensive genius when he opted for nearly two dozen plays of 20 personnel featuring Williams and Corum in the backfield together.

It was another reminder that Los Angeles doesn't need a healthy collection of superstar receivers to move the football when its running game is clicking.

Over a recent three-game winning stretch, the pair combined for 424 rushing yards, averaging 4.9 yards per carry. The Rams averaged 29 rushing attempts between them during those victories.

"We're just being able to get comfortable," Corum said. "It's one thing to watch film, but to actually be out there and feel the defense and figure out what they do, how they tackle, how you can set your blocks, how you can get them to trigger and then you bounce around."

The Cardinals game provided the clearest evidence yet that Corum deserves a bigger spotlight. But Williams' quieter afternoon — 23 rushing yards on 12 carries — shouldn't erase what he's delivered all season.

Los Angeles Rams running back Kyren Williams (23) runs the ball against Las Vegas Raiders cornerback Hezekiah Masses (35) during the second quarter of an NFL football game at Allegiant Stadium in Las Vegas, Nevada, Sunday, Oct. 25, 2026. Getty Images

Together, the two have given McVay something more valuable than a single workhorse: a backfield that can absorb injuries elsewhere, control the clock and keep Stafford from having to rescue the offense every Sunday.

With Green Bay looming on Thanksgiving Eve and the NFC playoff race tightening, that identity could determine how far Los Angeles goes.

Williams and Corum might not dominate the national conversation like the NFL's high-profile running backs.

But if being underrated means doing the dirty work while somebody else gets the headlines, the Rams have two of the best examples in football.

#2747. How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch

How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

As he overtook Jason Kidd for the third-most assists in NBA history, LeBron James piloted the Sixers' offense down the stretch against the Indiana Pacers on Sunday.

With Joel Embiid sitting out and Tyrese Maxey getting a breather on the bench, James took over in the fourth quarter. He did so by spamming the same action over and over again.

The set consisted of James getting a ball screen from a guard – either VJ Edgecombe or Anfernee Simons – at the top of the key. After setting or ghosting the screen, the guard would short roll towards the basket.

When the Pacers blitzed or hedged the screen, James found the guard. The Sixers could then attack a 4-on-3 or 3-on-2 situation.

Twice, Edgecombe and Simons scored off the roll.

When the Pacers helped to the roller, that opened up the shooters in each corner.

James directed traffic on offense to ensure that the Sixers spaced the floor properly. Here, he waved Dean Wade out of the dunker spot and into the corner. Edgecombe promptly hit him for an open three off the short roll.

After all of the Sixers' success, the Pacers eventually switched the ball screen. That left the 6-foot-4, 173-pound Quenton Jackson on James, who has five inches and 77 pounds on him.

Jackson and James had already gone head to head on Sunday.

In the first quarter, James set a ball screen for Edgecombe which the Pacers switched. Edgecombe fed James in the post and the Pacers doubled him to support Jackson. James punished them for it.

They didn't double James after the switch in the fourth quarter. This time, James backed Jackson down, backed him down again and backed him down some more before scoring inside.

James' strength abusing matchups against smaller defenders after switches has been a safety valve for the Sixers' offense. Guards struggle to stop him from getting to the basket. When defenses send help, he picks them apart with his otherworldly passing ability.

That combination forced the Pacers to pick their poison. From the 9:55 mark in the fourth until the Pacers switched to a zone defense (after James bullied Jackson) with just over 6:00 left, the Sixers scored 13 points out of this action across seven possessions. That's a stellar 1.86 points per possession.

James, who's scoring a career-low 15.1 points per game so far this season, might not be the elite bucket-getter he was for most of his career. But he's still brilliant at conducting highly efficient offense that pressures a defense's pain points.

#2746. Palo Alto inks $4.5 billion deal for AI startup Applied Compute, shares pop 7% on earnings

Palo Alto Networks shares jumped more than 7% after the cybersecurity firm beat Wall Street's fiscal first quarter estimates and inked a deal for artificial intelligence startup Applied Compute.

Here's how the company did versus LSEG estimates:

Revenue jumped 39% from $2.47 billion in the year-ago quarter.

Fears of AI-powered cyberattacks, heightened by incidents like the Hugging Face breach, are driving demand for cybersecurity vendors like Palo Alto Networks. Amid this backdrop, shares have jumped 85% this year.

"As AI reshapes the threat landscape, we must continually evolve to protect our customers and enable them to adopt AI securely," CEO Nikesh Arora said in a release.

Palo Alto said annual recurring revenue for its next-generation security business jumped 66% from a year ago to $9.73 billion. Arora said the NGS ARR was one of the highest beats in the company's history.

The cybersecurity company bumped up guidance for fiscal year 2027 as business spend more on security in the age of AI.

Palo Alto expects adjusted earnings per share in the range of $4.26 to 4.30 and revenue in the range of $14.52 billion to 14.57 billion. The forecasts topped the earnings of $4.18 per share and $14.16 billion in revenue expected by analysts.

Net income totaled $482 million, or 57 cents per share, up from $334 million, or 47 cents per share, a year ago.

Palo Alto Networks has been steadily acquiring companies to capitalize on demand for cyber tools in the age of AI.

Arora said the company's $4.5 billion all-stock deal for Applied Compute will help businesses secure open-weight models as adoption grows. The startup helps businesses run and customize their own AI models and marks a major investment for Palo Alto beyond cybersecurity.

"If it works, I think this will dwarf the ability that we have in cybersecurity," Arora said on an earnings call. "This could be bigger than what we do."

Outages from Microsoft, banks and the FAA have heightened concerns around cyberattacks over the last week. U.S. officials have so far ruled out any malicious activity, but experts say the rapid acceleration in patching is straining dated systems.

As businesses discover more vulnerabilities, Arora said the company expects tens of thousands of virtual patches by the end of the fiscal year. "Whatever they do today is going to take three to six months, maybe nine months, depending on how complex and how tired their environment is," Arora said.

#2743. Observations after Embiid posts season-high 37 points in Sixers' win over Nets

Observations after Embiid posts season-high 37 points in Sixers' win over Nets originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Joel Embiid's season-best 37 points led the Sixers to victory on Monday night in Brooklyn.

The Sixers notched a 135-123 win over the Nets to move to 12-3 on the season. Brooklyn fell to 3-10.

Jaylen Brown tied his season high with 31 points and set a new season high with 13 rebounds. Tyrese Maxey had 24 points and nine assists.

Julius Randle's 33 points topped the Nets.

On the second night of a back-to-back, the Sixers were without LeBron James (lower back/sciatica management), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear). The Sixers are now 4-0 without James.

They'll visit the Magic on Wednesday night. Here are observations on the Sixers' win against the Nets:

Embiid returned after missing Sunday's victory against the Pacers with the "right foot plantar fascia injury management" injury designation that's become the norm for him early this season.

He had an especially aggressive start in New York and set plenty of middle ball screens for Maxey. When the Nets blitzed or hard hedged Maxey, Embiid found a nice soft spot around the foul line and wasn't hesitant to fire open jumpers. The 32-year-old big man posted 13 points in the first quarter on 6-for-11 shooting (1 for 2 from three-point range).

Beyond the stats, it must be encouraging for the Sixers to watch Embiid trust his instincts and jumper. We'll see whether he can sustain a good run and fully snap out of his shooting slump.

Maxey broke the scoring ice when he swiped a steal and then coasted in for a dunk. Dean Wade's put-back dunk gave the Sixers a 4-0 lead. Wade started in James' place for the fourth time and had eight points and 11 rebounds over 32 minutes.

With James out and Embiid back, Jabari Walker played at both power forward and center. He logged 16 minutes, a number that would've been higher without his foul trouble. Walker scored 12 points and grabbed five rebounds, the latest in a string of strong performances.

The Sixers extended their lead at the start of the second quarter.

Anfernee Simons drilled a corner three. The Sixers' three-man bench shot 6 for 9 from the floor in the first half.

Everyone who played for the Sixers chipped in something positive. Wade leapt up to snag a Walker miss and threw down an and-one dunk. VJ Edgecombe hit a floater to grow the Sixers' advantage to 50-30. Embiid had a well-worn path to the foul line in the second quarter and made 9 of his 10 free throws.

Sixers head coach Nick Nurse kept an eight-man rotation, only playing Simons, Walker and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope off the bench until the final 18.5 seconds.

The Sixers' collective effort on the glass was a major strength in the first half.

All of the starters besides Maxey recorded at least one offensive rebound. Brown had a double-double before halftime and poured in 19 points over the first two quarters.

The Sixers' focus and intensity slipped in the third quarter.

Nolan Traore's layup cut Brooklyn's deficit to single digits. Michael Porter Jr. hit a long two over Caldwell-Pope to trim the Sixers' lead to six points.

The Sixers concluded the third period on a high note. Walker sunk a buzzer-beating three to lift his team to a 105-95 edge. Again, the 24-year-old provided much more than intangible energy and made meaningful, momentum-shifting plays.

The action stopped with 9:02 left in the fourth quarter after Brown was fouled by Randle. The Sixers' star forward appeared to have his forehead collide with Randle's face. He was down for several minutes before going to the bench.

Randle needed stitches in his mouth before re-entering with 5:01 to go, Amy Fadool reported on the NBC Sports Philadelphia broadcast. Brown subbed back in with 6:02 remaining and a bandage on the cut above his left eye. However, he exited at the 1:44 mark and did not return.

Nurse told reporters in Brooklyn that Brown was out at the end of the game because of "tightness," according to PhillyVoice's Adam Aaronson.

After their shaky third quarter, the Sixers were ultimately fine in the fourth and reached a dozen wins.

Egor Demin's jumper pulled the Nets to within 125-121, but Embiid then iced the game by swishing a corner three.

#2742. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2741. Nvidia interim CEO Kress tells employees ‘let’s move forward’ at first all-hands meeting since Jensen Huang exit

Interim Nvidia CEO Colette Kress told employees "let's move forward" on Wednesday during the company's first all-hands meeting since founder Jensen Huang resigned, a person who attended the meeting told CNBC. The person asked not to be named because the meeting was private.

Kress was joined by newly appointed board chair Harvey Jones and hyperscale and high-performance computing head Ian Buck.

Several other senior members of Nvidia's executive team spoke or joined Kress on stage, including longtime company veteran Jeff Fisher, senior vice president of software engineering Dwight Diercks, senior vice president of GPU engineering Jonah Alben, executive vice president in charge of operations Debora Shoquist and co-founder Chris Malachowsky, according to people familiar with the matter.

Kress opened with the message about moving forward and also mentioned pay raises and explained that the yearly review process, when employees typically get pay raises and are granted more stock, would be moved up from March to December. Nvidia shares are down 37% from their 52-week high.

Jones discussed the search for the next CEO, the person who attended the meeting said. The meeting lasted about an hour and was in person as well as streamed on Microsoft Teams.

The general sentiment swirling in the room, according to the person, was that employees want Huang to come back. Employees have spoken up on X in recent days to call for the return of Huang as CEO, and one senior employee reportedly sent a letter to the board warning Nvidia would lose "irreplaceable talent" if it doesn't consider reinstating Huang.

But another person familiar with the matter disagreed, saying that employees welcomed the remarks from leadership as well as the visibility into company leadership. The person asked not to be named because the matter is private.

"The Nvidia board unanimously accepted his resignation and does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO," Jones said during the company's earnings call on Tuesday.

Some executives shared that they do not agree with Jones. "Why make such a strong definitive statement when the investigation is still playing out," an employee told CNBC. The employee asked not to be named given the sensitivity of the subject.

The meeting was held one day after Huang said he was ready to return as CEO should the board want him. Multiple employees told CNBC that Huang's letter "fired employees up."

Despite recent turmoil, Wednesday's meeting was not out of the ordinary. Nvidia typically holds all-hands the morning after quarterly earnings reports, according to a former employee, who asked not to be named because the matter is private. Kress recapped Tuesday's earnings report, calling it a "great quarter," according to the person who said employees welcomed the remarks and visibility from leadership.

In past meetings, Huang took time to answer questions submitted by employees, according to the former employee. A person familiar with the matter told CNBC that the executives also answered pre-submitted questions in Wednesday's all-hands.

Nvidia shares were down Wednesday, a day after the company reported third-quarter earnings that topped Wall Street expectations and gave no signal that the company was going to slow its growth.

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that executives at Nvidia's all-hands meeting answered employee questions. A previous version mischaracterized how executives handled employee questions.
